Yeah, um… I guess what finally got me to agree to come in is that I just don’t really care about the things that used to keep me level. Like sailing races—I used to plan my whole week around those—and my Saturday shift with the historical society, and even messing around in the woodshop.

I mean, I’ve got a half-finished little cabinet out there that I keep telling myself I’ll get back to, but then I walk past it like it’s just another chore. Same with the sailing stuff, the gear’s just sitting there. It’s odd, because those things used to be what kept me balanced, you know? Now I keep putting them off.

Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.

Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.

I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.

It’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.

Same with the sailing stuff, the gear’s just sitting there. It’s odd, because those things used to be what kept me balanced, you know? Now I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly? I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more like this low-grade dread that just follows me around. And guilt, too—like I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n\nI know that sounds like project-management talk, sorry. I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment period. Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. Like my wife and kids will be laughing at dinner, and I can tell it’s funny, I can tell it’s one of those normal family moments you’re supposed to kind of soak in… but I mostly just feel flat, like I’m watching it from a little distance.\n\nSame thing at work. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything—our scale at home is one of those digital ones that gives you a different number if you move it two inches, so I don’t really trust it. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I’d need you to say what you mean by “thought about death or dying.” Like, just the idea of it? Or something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like hurting myself. Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I’ve had moments where I think, “I’m tired of feeling like this,” but that’s not the same thing as wanting to die, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a perfectly clean answer. My mind goes to, like, people saying things casually—“I could disappear for a week”—and they don’t mean it literally. For me it’s more like wanting everything to quiet down, not wanting to be dead.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Recurring thoughts about death… I don’t think so, not in a way I’d call recurring.

I mean, death comes up in the normal ways, I guess—news stories, someone’s parent getting ill, those awful fundraising emails with a tragic story attached. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what finally got me to agree to come in is that I just don’t really care about the things that used to keep me level. Like sailing races—I used to plan my whole week around those—and my Saturday shift with the historical society, and even messing around in the woodshop.\n\nI mean, I’ve got a half-finished little cabinet out there that I keep telling myself I’ll get back to, but then I walk past it like it’s just another chore. Same with the sailing stuff, the gear’s just sitting there. It’s odd, because those things used to be what kept me balanced, you know? Now I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly? I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more like this low-grade dread that just follows me around. And guilt, too—like I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n\nI know that sounds like project-management talk, sorry. I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment period. Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. Like my wife and kids will be laughing at dinner, and I can tell it’s funny, I can tell it’s one of those normal family moments you’re supposed to kind of soak in… but I mostly just feel flat, like I’m watching it from a little distance.\n\nSame thing at work. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything—our scale at home is one of those digital ones that gives you a different number if you move it two inches, so I don’t really trust it. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I’d need you to say what you mean by “thought about death or dying.” Like, just the idea of it? Or something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like hurting myself. Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I’ve had moments where I think, “I’m tired of feeling like this,” but that’s not the same thing as wanting to die, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a perfectly clean answer. My mind goes to, like, people saying things casually—“I could disappear for a week”—and they don’t mean it literally. For me it’s more like wanting everything to quiet down, not wanting to be dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recurring thoughts about death, even if they're not about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts about death… I don’t think so, not in a way I’d call recurring.\n\nI mean, death comes up in the normal ways, I guess—news stories, someone’s parent getting ill, those awful fundraising emails with a tragic story attached. And then I’ll think about it for a minute, sure. But I’m not sitting around having the same death thoughts over and over, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may be over-parsing your question. I do that at work too—someone asks a simple thing and I turn it into three categories and a spreadsheet. But no, I haven’t noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s actually what finally pushed me to come here, if I’m honest.

It’s not that I can’t feel any pleasure at all, exactly, but the things that used to kind of reset me just don’t seem to have the same pull. Sailing races, for example—I used to look forward to those all week. Now I’ll see the email about sign-ups and think, “I should do that,” and then I just… don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society, which sounds boring to some people, I know, but I liked it. Even tinkering around in the woodshop feels like another chore I’m avoiding.

I still tell myself I’ll get back to them, but lately it’s like I don’t care enough to actually start.

Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. Like my wife and kids will be laughing at dinner, and I can tell it’s funny, I can tell it’s one of those normal family moments you’re supposed to kind of soak in… but I mostly just feel flat, like I’m watching it from a little distance.\n\nSame thing at work. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment period. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I've gotten very good at routing my day so I don't have to go near that coastal site, the harbor walkways by the cranes, or conversations where someone starts replaying what went wrong.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I can remember walking the site and follows me around, like I should have seen one more risk coming even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner or I can finish a difficult project milestone, and I know those things should feel good, but mostly I just feel flat and a little removed from it.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n  description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: A few times I've taken the boat out alone when the weather was turning, not exactly hoping for trouble, but ignoring the kind of judgment I used to pride myself on having.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If someone drops a metal ladder or a truck gate slams, I jump hard before I can stop myself, and it takes me longer than it should to get my heart rate back down.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still doing my job, but I’ve started delegating site visits I should be leading and avoiding client discussions that touch the accident, which makes me worry I’m not giving the project the steady direction it needs.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n yourself?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts about death… I don’t think so, not in a way I’d call recurring.\n\nI mean, death comes up in the normal ways, I guess—news stories, someone’s parent getting ill, those awful fundraising emails with a tragic story attached. And then I’ll think about it for a minute, sure. But I’m not sitting around having the same death thoughts over and over, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may be over-parsing your question. I do that at work too—someone asks a simple thing and I turn it into three categories and a spreadsheet. But no, I haven’t noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually what finally pushed me to come here, if I’m honest.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t feel any pleasure at all, exactly, but the things that used to kind of reset me just don’t seem to have the same pull. Sailing races, for example—I used to look forward to those all week. Now I’ll see the email about sign-ups and think, “I should do that,” and then I just… don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society, which sounds boring to some people, I know, but I liked it. Even tinkering around in the woodshop feels like another chore I’m avoiding.\n\nI still tell myself I’ll get back to them, but lately it’s like I don’t care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Has your mental state changed at all over the past year, or has it stayed relatively the same?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what finally got me to agree to come in is that I just don’t really care about the things that used to keep me level. Like sailing races—I used to plan my whole week around those—and my Saturday shift with the historical society, and even messing around in the woodshop.\n\nI mean, I’ve got a half-finished little cabinet out there that I keep telling myself I’ll get back to, but then I walk past it like it’s just another chore. Same with the sailing stuff, the gear’s just sitting there. It’s odd, because those things used to be what kept me balanced, you know? Now I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly? I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more like this low-grade dread that just follows me around. And guilt, too—like I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n\nI know that sounds like project-management talk, sorry. I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment period. Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. Like my wife and kids will be laughing at dinner, and I can tell it’s funny, I can tell it’s one of those normal family moments you’re supposed to kind of soak in… but I mostly just feel flat, like I’m watching it from a little distance.\n\nSame thing at work. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything—our scale at home is one of those digital ones that gives you a different number if you move it two inches, so I don’t really trust it. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I’d need you to say what you mean by “thought about death or dying.” Like, just the idea of it? Or something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like hurting myself. Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I’ve had moments where I think, “I’m tired of feeling like this,” but that’s not the same thing as wanting to die, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a perfectly clean answer. My mind goes to, like, people saying things casually—“I could disappear for a week”—and they don’t mean it literally. For me it’s more like wanting everything to quiet down, not wanting to be dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recurring thoughts about death, even if they're not about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts about death… I don’t think so, not in a way I’d call recurring.\n\nI mean, death comes up in the normal ways, I guess—news stories, someone’s parent getting ill, those awful fundraising emails with a tragic story attached. And then I’ll think about it for a minute, sure. But I’m not sitting around having the same death thoughts over and over, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may be over-parsing your question. I do that at work too—someone asks a simple thing and I turn it into three categories and a spreadsheet. But no, I haven’t noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually what finally pushed me to come here, if I’m honest.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t feel any pleasure at all, exactly, but the things that used to kind of reset me just don’t seem to have the same pull. Even tinkering around in the woodshop feels like another chore I’m avoiding.\n\nI still tell myself I’ll get back to them, but lately it’s like I don’t care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Has your mental state changed at all over the past year, or has it stayed relatively the same?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. Now I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly? I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more like this low-grade dread that just follows me around. And guilt, too—like I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n\nI know that sounds like project-management talk, sorry. I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment period. Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. Like my wife and kids will be laughing at dinner, and I can tell it’s funny, I can tell it’s one of those normal family moments you’re supposed to kind of soak in… but I mostly just feel flat, like I’m watching it from a little distance.\n\nSame thing at work. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything—our scale at home is one of those digital ones that gives you a different number if you move it two inches, so I don’t really trust it. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I’d need you to say what you mean by “thought about death or dying.” Like, just the idea of it? Or something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like hurting myself. Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I’ve had moments where I think, “I’m tired of feeling like this,” but that’s not the same thing as wanting to die, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a perfectly clean answer. My mind goes to, like, people saying things casually—“I could disappear for a week”—and they don’t mean it literally. For me it’s more like wanting everything to quiet down, not wanting to be dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recurring thoughts about death, even if they're not about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts about death… I don’t think so, not in a way I’d call recurring.\n\nI mean, death comes up in the normal ways, I guess—news stories, someone’s parent getting ill, those awful fundraising emails with a tragic story attached. And then I’ll think about it for a minute, sure. But I’m not sitting around having the same death thoughts over and over, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may be over-parsing your question. I do that at work too—someone asks a simple thing and I turn it into three categories and a spreadsheet. But no, I haven’t noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually what finally pushed me to come here, if I’m honest.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t feel any pleasure at all, exactly, but the things that used to kind of reset me just don’t seem to have the same pull. Sailing races, for example—I used to look forward to those all week. Now I’ll see the email about sign-ups and think, “I should do that,” and then I just… don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society, which sounds boring to some people, I know, but I liked it. Even tinkering around in the woodshop feels like another chore I’m avoiding.\n\nI still tell myself I’ll get back to them, but lately it’s like I don’t care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Has your mental state changed at all over the past year, or has it stayed relatively the same?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I keep thinking of it as “since the accident,” because that’s sort of the marker in my head.\n\nThere was that scaffold accident during the business readjustment period—everything at work was already kind of chaotic then, people changing roles, meetings about restructuring, you know how companies put polite words on stressful things. And after that, things didn’t really go back to normal for me.\n\nSo… a little over a year now, I’d say. It hasn’t been the same the whole time exactly, but that’s when I’d date the change from.\n\nDoctor: Has your mental state been consistently the same since the scaffold accident, or have you noticed any fluctuations?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t been exactly the same day to day, no. I mean, some weeks I can sort of function and convince myself it’s just stress or getting older or whatever.\n\nBut the overall thing has been there since the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment mess at work. That’s the line I keep coming back to. So… a little over a year now, I guess. The intensity shifts around, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of dread, guilt, and loss of interest in activities for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. I keep sort of anchoring it to that scaffold accident, during the whole business readjustment thing at work—funny phrase, “readjustment,” like it was just moving chairs around, but it felt bigger than that.\n\nSo… a little over a year now. That’s when I’d say all this really started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like sailing races—I used to plan my whole week around those—and my Saturday shift with the historical society, and even messing around in the woodshop.\n\nI mean, I’ve got a half-finished little cabinet out there that I keep telling myself I’ll get back to, but then I walk past it like it’s just another chore. Same with the sailing stuff, the gear’s just sitting there. It’s odd, because those things used to be what kept me balanced, you know? Now I keep putting them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly? I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more like this low-grade dread that just follows me around. And guilt, too—like I should’ve seen one more risk coming, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n\nI know that sounds like project-management talk, sorry. I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. Since the scaffold accident, during that business readjustment period. Funny, I still think of it in terms of the company timeline—like “before the readjustment” and “after”—which probably sounds strange. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. I mean, food is kind of whatever, but the bigger thing is I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me steady. Sailing races, my Saturday thing at the historical society, even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around—it all feels like another task on the list now.\n\nI used to get a lot out of that stuff. I’d spend half the week thinking about the boat or some little project I wanted to fix, and now I just keep putting it off. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. Like my wife and kids will be laughing at dinner, and I can tell it’s funny, I can tell it’s one of those normal family moments you’re supposed to kind of soak in… but I mostly just feel flat, like I’m watching it from a little distance.\n\nSame thing at work. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything—our scale at home is one of those digital ones that gives you a different number if you move it two inches, so I don’t really trust it. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I’d need you to say what you mean by “thought about death or dying.” Like, just the idea of it? Or something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like hurting myself. Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I’ve had moments where I think, “I’m tired of feeling like this,” but that’s not the same thing as wanting to die, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a perfectly clean answer. My mind goes to, like, people saying things casually—“I could disappear for a week”—and they don’t mean it literally. For me it’s more like wanting everything to quiet down, not wanting to be dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recurring thoughts about death, even if they're not about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts about death… I don’t think so, not in a way I’d call recurring.\n\nI mean, death comes up in the normal ways, I guess—news stories, someone’s parent getting ill, those awful fundraising emails with a tragic story attached. And then I’ll think about it for a minute, sure. But I’m not sitting around having the same death thoughts over and over, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may be over-parsing your question. I do that at work too—someone asks a simple thing and I turn it into three categories and a spreadsheet. But no, I haven’t noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually what finally pushed me to come here, if I’m honest.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t feel any pleasure at all, exactly, but the things that used to kind of reset me just don’t seem to have the same pull. Sailing races, for example—I used to look forward to those all week. Now I’ll see the email about sign-ups and think, “I should do that,” and then I just… don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society, which sounds boring to some people, I know, but I liked it. Even tinkering around in the woodshop feels like another chore I’m avoiding.\n\nI still tell myself I’ll get back to them, but lately it’s like I don’t care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Has your mental state changed at all over the past year, or has it stayed relatively the same?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I keep thinking of it as “since the accident,” because that’s sort of the marker in my head.\n\nThere was that scaffold accident during the business readjustment period—everything at work was already kind of chaotic then, people changing roles, meetings about restructuring, you know how companies put polite words on stressful things. And after that, things didn’t really go back to normal for me.\n\nSo… a little over a year now, I’d say. It hasn’t been the same the whole time exactly, but that’s when I’d date the change from.\n\nDoctor: Has your mental state been consistently the same since the scaffold accident, or have you noticed any fluctuations?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t been exactly the same day to day, no. I mean, some weeks I can sort of function and convince myself it’s just stress or getting older or whatever.\n\nBut the overall thing has been there since the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment mess at work. That’s the line I keep coming back to. So… a little over a year now, I guess. The intensity shifts around, but it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of dread, guilt, and loss of interest in activities for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No, not more than two years. I keep sort of anchoring it to that scaffold accident, during the whole business readjustment thing at work—funny phrase, “readjustment,” like it was just moving chairs around, but it felt bigger than that.\n\nSo… a little over a year now. That’s when I’d say all this really started.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No, not two years or more. It’s more like… a little over a year. I keep measuring it from that scaffold accident, during the business readjustment at work. Which is funny, because they called it “readjustment” like it was some neat little office shuffle, but it didn’t feel neat at all.\n\nSo, yeah—since then. A little over a year, not two.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the part that finally got me to agree to come in was less some dramatic moment and more realizing I just don’t care about the things that used to sort of keep me level.\n\nLike sailing races—I used to plan around those. And my Saturday shift at the historical society, I used to enjoy that, talking to people about old maps and local buildings and all that. Even going out to the woodshop and tinkering around… now it all feels like chores I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’ll go next week, or I’ll just clean up the bench first, and then I don’t.\n\nSo, nearly every day? Yeah, it’s been pretty consistently there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… most of the day, I’d say. It’s not like every single minute is identical, but it’s there in the background almost all the time.

I keep thinking in terms of what I missed, what I should’ve caught. It’s not crying-all-day sad, it’s more… carrying this weight around.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year, I guess. But yeah, that’s when it started, and it just hasn’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy things you used to find pleasurable, like eating your favorite foods or spending time with loved ones?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really. Like it should still be enjoyable, but it mostly feels like a chore.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. That’s kind of what finally made me agree to come in—I realized I wasn’t just “busy” or tired of my routine. The things that used to keep me balanced don’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nLike the sailing races… I still get the emails and I think, “I should sign up,” and then I just don’t. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being around all those old records and displays—funny, they had this whole thing once about early harbor construction, which I could talk about too long—but now it feels like something I’m supposed to do, not something I look forward to.\n\nEven the woodshop. I’ll walk past a half-finished little project and think I should get out there, but it just feels like another chore I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you joy or give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean, there are moments where I know I should feel something good. If I finish a difficult project milestone—something that would’ve made me feel proud before—I know, logically, it’s a good thing. I might even say the right thing in the meeting, you know, “good job everyone,” but inside it doesn’t really land. It’s just… muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. That’s really the thing that pushed me to come in, honestly. It wasn’t one big dramatic moment, it was more like realizing I just don’t care about the stuff that used to keep me balanced.\n\nThe sailing races, my Saturday shift at the historical society, even just messing around in the woodshop… I used to look forward to those. Now I’ll think about doing them and then just put it off, like it’s laundry or paperwork. Funny, the woodshop still has this little half-finished thing sitting there—nothing important, but I used to enjoy that sort of tinkering. Now it just feels like another chore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything—our scale at home is one of those digital ones that gives you a different number if you move it two inches, so I don’t really trust it. But my clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell. Nothing where I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve really gained or lost a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… anxious maybe isn’t the exact word all the time, but on edge, yes. It’s like this low-grade dread that just sort of follows me around.\n\nAnd guilt, too. I’ll be doing something normal—checking an email, driving past a construction site, whatever—and my brain starts turning over what I should’ve seen coming. One more risk, one more control, one more thing I should’ve caught. People keep telling me the right controls were in place, that I couldn’t have predicted everything, but it doesn’t really… stick, I guess. It’s just there in the background most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in a clear way, if that’s what you mean. I think about people dying sometimes, but I suppose everybody does, right? Especially when you get to my age and you start noticing which parents are getting sick, who’s retiring, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s funny, my dad used to read the obituaries every morning like it was the sports page—drove my mother crazy. I catch myself doing little things like that now, and then I think, well, that’s cheerful. But… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I’d need you to say what you mean by “thought about death or dying.” Like, just the idea of it? Or something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’ve had thoughts like hurting myself. Wishing I were dead— that sounds so stark when you say it that way. I’ve had moments where I think, “I’m tired of feeling like this,” but that’s not the same thing as wanting to die, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a perfectly clean answer. My mind goes to, like, people saying things casually—“I could disappear for a week”—and they don’t mean it literally. I’d call it mild, I guess… I mean, I’m still working. I’m not falling apart or anything like that.

But it is getting in the way more than I like to admit. I’ve started handing off site visits that I should really be leading myself, and I’ll avoid client conversations if I think they’re going to circle anywhere near the accident. Which is awkward, because in project management, half the job is being the steady person in the room.

It reminds me of when a sailboat’s just a little off trim—you’re still moving, but you can feel it pulling wrong the whole time. So… mild, maybe, but not nothing. It’s affecting how I do my job.
